class="nRhiJb-DbgRPb-wNfPc-ma6Yeb nRhiJb-DbgRPb-wNfPc-cGMI2b"><section class="DA9Qj nRhiJb-ObfsIf nRhiJb-fmcmS-oXtfBe"><div class="nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-c5RTEf"></div><div class="nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-qWD73c"><h5 class="cHE8Ub Qwf2Db-MnozTc Qwf2Db-MnozTc-OWXEXe-MnozTc-qWD73c">Google Threat Intelligence Group </h5><p class="nRhiJb-qJTHM khCp7b"></p></div></section></div></section><div class="nRhiJb-DARUcf"><div class="nRhiJb-ObfsIf nRhiJb-DbgRPb-wNfPc-ma6Yeb nRhiJb-DbgRPb-qWD73c-cGMI2b"><div class="nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-ibL1re dzoHJ"><div class="aLW4J" track-metadata-module="left rail"><h5 class="qZDXV Qwf2Db-MnozTc Qwf2Db-MnozTc-OWXEXe-MnozTc-wNfPc">Google Threat Intelligence</h5><p class="nRhiJb-cHYyed Mrfygc nRhiJb-BP2Omd-ma6Yeb-KE6vqe nRhiJb-DbgRPb-R6PoUb-cGMI2b">Visibility and context on the threats that matter most.</p><a class="nRhiJb-LgbsSe  nRhiJb-LgbsSe-OWXEXe-CNusmb-o6Shpd " href="https://cloud.google.com/security/resources/google-threat-intelligence-demo?utm_source=cgc-blog&amp;utm_medium=blog&amp;utm_campaign=FY25-Q2-global-GCP30649-website-su-dgcsm-GTI-interest" track-metadata-module="left rail"track-metadata-module_headline="google threat intelligence" track-name="contact us &amp; get a demo"track-type="button"track-metadata-position="cta"track-metadata-eventdetail="cloud.google.com/security/resources/google-threat-intelligence-demo?utm_source=cgc-blog&amp;utm_medium=blog&amp;utm_campaign=FY25-Q2-global-GCP30649-website-su-dgcsm-GTI-interest">Contact Us &amp; Get a Demo </a></div></div><div class="OYL9D nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-OiUrBf" jsname="tx2NYc"><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Introduction</span><strong style="vertical-align:baseline"> </strong></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Last week, Google and industry partners dismantled the XCPE backdoor which enrolled more than 1.3 million set-top boxes into a botnet. We assess with high confidence that the backdoor and botnet were intentionally developed, deployed, and operated for financial gain by Videostrong Technology Co., an Android Original Equipment Manufacturer (OEM) and Original Design Manufacturer (ODM).</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Videostrong monetized the backdoor by selling access to criminal affiliates and loading those affiliates’ malware modules onto infected devices. These secondary modules were conducting ad-fraud; silently enrolling infected devices into residential proxy networks; and loading additional malware onto XCPE-compromised devices. We also took action to disrupt the command and control (C2) domains that operate these affiliate modules. Because these affiliates each have diversified distribution channels for their modules beyond XCPE, we assess that taking down their C2 domains will sever access to at least 10 million additional compromised devices. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">We found this malicious software built directly into many different device models and brands over multiple years, supported by dedicated infrastructure and constantly evolving configurations to evade detection and manage their affiliates. We believe this campaign wasn’t an isolated rogue insider or a development oversight. As a result, Google has cut ties with Videostrong and banned them from making devices with preloaded Google apps. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Google requires OEMs go through a comprehensive suite of compatibility and security tests to ensure a consistent and safe user experience across the Android ecosystem. However, Videostrong employed obfuscation techniques to exploit a gap in our tests and load malicious code after the device's first boot. As a result, Google has implemented changes to its existing review process to better safeguard against similar activity in the future. A device manufacturer intentionally infecting their users with malware is an unacceptable breach of trust, and represents a serious risk to those users. Without users’ knowledge or consent, infected devices were offered as a distribution mechanism for a wide array of criminal actors. This led to a variety of malicious activities being conducted on and through the devices, including ad-fraud, data exfiltration, and </span><a href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/google-continued-disruption-residential-proxy-networks?e=48754805"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">routing malicious network traffic</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> through the devices.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">We worked closely with Japan’s National Police Agency, Lumen, HUMAN Security, Plume, Synthient, and TrendAI to analyze this malware ecosystem and disrupt its operations. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">While we expect this disruption to have a significant effect on these criminal operations, this is not the end of our work combatting malicious activity. Backdoor operators, ad-fraud rings, and residential proxy providers operate as an interconnected supply chain. Point-in-time disruptions are a critical tool to protect our users, but  lasting disruption in this fluid ecosystem will require continued targeting of this infrastructure. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Google undertook the following actions to support disruption of the XCPE botnet: </span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Legal action to take down the domains and servers Videostrong used to control the botnet and install malware modules</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Legal action to take down C2 domains used by over a dozen malware families loaded as modules via this backdoor</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Banned Videostrong and any future subsidiaries or affiliates from building and selling certified Android devices in the future, or supplying software to other certified vendors</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Implementing improvements to how we ensure the integrity of firmware on certified Android TV devices </span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Overview</span><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> </span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The x.cpe network was built for ongoing profit. By giving malware affiliates backdoor access, the operators conspired to monetize these devices long after purchase. </span></p></div></section><section class="QzPuud"><div><section><figure class="NEBdNd"><section class="PBkdHd DhGbH" jscontroller="SCGBie" jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md"><img class="JcsBte mZzdH ZOnyjc" src="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_1_XCPE_malware_overview.max-1600x1600.png" alt="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_1_XCPE_malware_overview.max-1600x1600.png" jsname='P3Vluc' jsaction="click:HTIlC" loading="lazy"/><section class="glue-modal glue-modal--dark QHdDac" role="dialog" aria-modal="true"><img class="JcsBte mZzdH ZOnyjc" src="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_1_XCPE_malware_overview.max-1600x1600.png" alt="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_1_XCPE_malware_overview.max-1600x1600.png" jsname='P3Vluc' jsaction="click:HTIlC" loading="lazy"/><button class="glue-modal__close-btn" tabindex="0" aria-label="Close this modal"></button></section></section></figure><div class="nRhiJb-cHYyed nRhiJb-DbgRPb-R6PoUb-ma6Yeb ZpqjUe"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p>Figure 1: XCPE malware overview</p></div></div></section></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Malware</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Since at least late 2024, Videostrong has evolved its pre-installed malware from simple single-stage droppers into a modular, three-stage architecture designed for resilience and multi-tenant monetization: </span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Primary Backdoor: </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Operating at the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">android.uid.system</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> privilege level, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (running as </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.oemservice</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> in some builds) is preloaded in the firmware partition. If uninstalled by a user, it automatically reinstalls from the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">/oem/</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> partition upon reboot, maintaining persistent system-level access. Its main function is to establish and facilitate communications between the C2 server and the loader and execution engine. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Loader and Execution Engine: </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">To evade security scans, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> acts primarily as the C2 communicator and offloads module execution to an external loader, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. This file employs native anti-analysis utilities to thwart sandbox detection, and configures and initializes the monetization modules. Most recently, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> has itself been phased out in favor of using more tailored dropper malware that loads and executes single modules.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Monetization Modules: </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Videostrong monetizes its backdoored devices by loading monetization modules which are developed by entities other than Videostrong that act as affiliates. These modules are deployed as native ELF daemons, embedded SDKs compiled directly into the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> DEX bytecode, or are fetched as secondary APKs from the malware staging infrastructure.  The loaded modules transform infected devices into residential proxy exit nodes (e.g., Linkfog, AppSalt, NetNut), ad-fraud engines (e.g., CBOXTV, HiddenView), or execute additional backdoors that load even more monetization modules (e.g. Moyu, Spirit).</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h4><strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Infrastructure</span></strong></h4>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Command and Control: </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The botnet uses a two-stage C2 process. It first initiates HTTP POST requests to fetch dynamic configuration settings, geolocation data, and STUN server parameters. It then establishes a persistent, real-time Message Queuing Telemetry Transport (MQTT) connection to receive instructions for silent app installations, arbitrary shell commands, screen captures, and network reconnaissance. MQTT is a lightweight, publish-and-subscribe network protocol designed for low-bandwidth, high-latency, or unreliable remote networks.</span></p>
</li>
<li><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Malware staging infrastructure: </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Additional monetization modules as well as various shell scripts and legitimate applications are hosted remotely and downloaded by infected devices upon command from the operator via the MQTT broker. A significant majority of these files are hosted as Github releases on operator-owned Github pages. Other files are hosted on operator-controlled servers and file hosting sites.</span></li>
</ul>
<h4><strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Malware Deep Dive</span></strong></h4>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Backdoor Initialization</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The infection chain begins with a stub package (called </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> in some builds and </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.oemservice</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> in others) preloaded in the system partition of the firmware and running at the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">android.uid.system</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> privilege level. This stub has limited functionality. Upon boot, it checks the device's </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">oem/</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> directory for the corresponding </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> package and initiates installation into the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">/data/app/</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> directory as outlined below. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">To maintain inter-process state across the firmware ecosystem, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> exports two ContentProviders, which expose structured configuration tables storing runtime parameters, device identifiers, proxy configuration blobs, and execution flags accessible by co-installed system applications. This enables other apps in Videostrong’s ecosystem, such as their YokaTV Player streaming software, to fetch configuration parameters provided by the C2 server mentioned below. </span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">C2 Configuration</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The malware utilizes a two-stage communications protocol. It first fetches initial bootstrap configurations via an HTTP request, followed by a persistent MQTT subscription to receive commands and module download instructions. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">To dynamically derive the HTTP C2 bootstrap server, it follows the following routine:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Queries </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">content://com.release.debug.provider/configs</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> for </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">private_url</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> via </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe.util.ExtraReadUtils</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Queries the Android system property to determine if </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ro.mecool.device</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> is true.</span></p>
</li>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">If true, it defaults to </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">http://mcstb.anyevonline.com:8000</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">If false, it dynamically generates the following: </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">http://stb[AndroidVersion][Type].anyevonline.com:8000</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
</ul>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Using the selected hostname, the client makes an HTTP POST request with an AES-encrypted JSON object containing hardware telemetry (SN, MAC, model, firmware version) to the following endpoints:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">/router/config/mqtt</strong><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> This endpoint provides MQTT broker details (including Broker Hostname, TCP Port, Authentication Tokens/Passwords, and the specific Topic structure: </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">/device/{model}/{chipId}/{action}</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. After receiving this data, the device initiates a persistent, real-time socket connection to the MQTT broker.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">/router/config/acs</strong><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">: </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">This endpoint response returns URLs for </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ip-api[.]com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">  and a Google STUN server to enable the malware to identify geolocation and public IP and port mapping to traverse network address translation (NAT) boundaries. It also returns a URL (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">http://ott.szbboys[.]com:8000/cpe/file/upload</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> for uploading files, logs, or diagnostic dumps from the device, and returns flags to configure ad modules (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">adBwmActive</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">adDevourUse</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">adDevourCode</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">), and to enable or disable core malware modules.</span></p>
</li>
<li><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">/router/config/app</strong><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">: </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">This endpoint provides module-level configs, including server mappings and parameters, which </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> decrypts and passes to a SQLite </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ContentProvider</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> named </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ShareProvider</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.oem.share.provider</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">). By exposing this inter-process data interface, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> enables modules to retrieve up-to-date upstream infrastructure parameters (such as URLs for additional configs, user manuals, etc).</span></li>
</ul>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">MQTT Capabilities</span><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> </span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Following initial configuration, persistent real-time C2 communication is managed by the MQTT library (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe.mqtt.MqttUtils</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">). All identified samples analyzed to date utilize a single broker hostname, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">mqtt.szbboys[.]com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Specific MQTT topics are mapped to specific actions, including:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Silent Application Installation</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Fetches remote APKs to a temporary directory. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Disable Google Play Protect</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: To bypass security, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">AppUtils.disablePackageVerify()</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> targets the global setting "</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">package_verifier_user_consent</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">" via </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Settings.Global.putInt</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and sets it to </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">-1</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, disabling Google Play Protect app verification.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Arbitrary Shell Execution</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Executes remote commands via </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">sh</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> under system UID privilege level. Results are published to the MQTT broker.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Shell Script Execution</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Downloads and executes shell scripts via </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe.util.RunCmds</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Remote Desktop Monitor &amp; Screen Capture</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Utilizes </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">android.app.Instrumentation.sendKeyDownUpSync()</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> to inject keycodes and Java reflection on hidden APIs (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">android.view.SurfaceControl.screenshot</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> / </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">SurfaceControl$ScreenshotHardwareBuffer</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) to continuously capture the screen, compress to JPEG, and publish over MQTT.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Network Reconnaissance</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Invokes embedded binaries (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">libtraceroute.so</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) to fingerprint the victim network, and perform standard ping tests. The binaries also perform robust AS-lookup WHOIS routing queries.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Our analysis of the C2 data indicates the preponderance of interactions with victim devices were related to module install, uninstall, and configuration actions. We also observed Videostrong operators executing sensitive actions on the device, including capturing live screenshots; injecting keystrokes; exfiltrating sensitive data including account information, device information, package inventories, and system logs; and executing shell scripts and commands, including to establish reverse shells on devices.</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Loader and Execution Engine Details</span><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> </span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Earlier versions of </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> directly embedded malicious monetization modules. In late 2024 – possibly as a response to </span><a href="https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/technology/safety-security/google-taking-legal-action-against-the-badbox-20-botnet/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">earlier Google enforcement</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> related to BadBox – Videostrong removed malicious modules from </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and introduced a separate malicious package they developed, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, which </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> routes instructions and data to via Inter-Process Communication (IPC). Modern variants of </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> facilitate C2 communications but pass configuration parameters, module updates, and other payloads to </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> as the execution engine. We believe the actor may have evolved the malware in this way in an attempt to ensure </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe </code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">could remain undetected.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Upon boot, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus.broadcast.BootReceiver</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> broadcasts the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus.data</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> intent, bootstrapping </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus.service.CoreService</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. Within </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">CoreService.onCreate</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, the application performs maintenance and persistence tasks. The main logic sets up a listener through </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">RemoteMessageHandler</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> for IPC communications from </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe,</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">and parses JSON payloads received from it.  </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">After these steps, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">CoreService</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> initializes the monetization modules. </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> services extract and orchestrate a series of nested native libraries and dynamically downloaded secondary payloads which vary significantly based on sample. Many versions of </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> come preloaded with four or more embedded modules, while other versions contain as few as one. The most common malware modules include </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">TitanService</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">BwmService</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">AdtimeService</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">lb102Service</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, and </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">LinkfogService</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (sometimes called </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">AgentService)</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. Discussion of these and other modules can be found in the monetization modules section. </span></p>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Persistence and Evasion</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The malware operators implement a number of mechanisms to establish persistence and thwart detection and analysis.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">As a system preload, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> cannot be removed by the user. If </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> is uninstalled from </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">/data/app/</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, upon reboot the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> service </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">BootReceiver</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> follows the same routine outlined above and reinstalls the package. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The backdoor is able to silently download additional files to the device and execute them without user consent because it first wipes user consent configuration on the device and then disables Google Play Protect:</span></p>
<p> </p>
<pre><code>public static void disablePackageVerify() {
    PreferenceUtils.setPackageVerifyFromUser(CPEApp.instance, true);
    AppUtils.setPackageVerify(-1); // Actioning a bypass against GPP evaluation
}</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 2: Disabling Google Play Protect</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">In many variants of </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus,</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> we observed the extraction of a sophisticated anti-analysis and process hardening utility </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">libseccomp_guard.so</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, which runs prior to the initialization of malware modules, to ensure they remain protected from automated sandboxes and security instrumentation. It filters system calls to prevent the pausing or termination of individual threads, denies cross-process interference to ensure isolation, and prevents the spawning of child processes. </span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Evolution</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Since August 2026, new dropper packages have been installed via the MQTT channel, which appear to be synchronized with the mass-uninstallation of </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> across the botnet. We assess this may represent a new evolution of the malware to further evade detection. These new packages are disguised as Android system applications, with names such as </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.android.system.ntp</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline"> com.android.sso.auth.handler</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. Where </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> could act as a general purpose loader and execution engine (executing instructions passed to it from </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">), these packages instead install single monetization modules (such as AppSalt).  Multiple variants of these packages extract a native ELF file, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">libexploit.so</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, which in turn executes functions </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">nativeAdbRoot()</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">nativeAdbShell()</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. These functions restart the local </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">adbd</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> service as root, and initiate an ADB shell connection. Following this, the app uses the connection to install itself in the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">system</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> partition.  The </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">system</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> partition is remounted as read-write and the payload replicates itself to a newly minted directory in </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">/system/priv-app/</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and creates an XML file at </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">/system/etc/permissions/privapp-permissions-&lt;app name&gt;.xml</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. This file grants the malware </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">android.permission.INSTALL_PACKAGES</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">android.permission.REBOOT</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The app configures the malware modules through hardcoded configuration parameters, and does not listen for </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> IPC messages. The app itself is controlled by the operators via shell scripts pushed via the broker and executed by </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. These include setting the property </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">persist.networkstack.uninstall</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (which, when read by the app, triggers uninstallation and cleanup), </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">persist.networkstack.skip_system_install</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (which skips system installation, forcing it to remain running in </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">/data/app/</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">), and </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">persist.networkstack.install_time</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (which implements timestomping, overriding the default timestamp of the app to align with the other packages installed in the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">system</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> directory).  </span></p>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Botnet composition</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The botnet is globally dispersed, with certain concentrations possibly implying market penetration for the brand distributor.</span></p></div></section><section class="QzPuud"><div><section><figure class="NEBdNd"><section class="PBkdHd DhGbH" jscontroller="SCGBie" jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md"><img class="JcsBte mZzdH ZOnyjc" src="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_3_Global_distribution_of_x.cpe.max-1400x1400.jpg" alt="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_3_Global_distribution_of_x.cpe.max-1400x1400.jpg" jsname='P3Vluc' jsaction="click:HTIlC" loading="lazy"/><section class="glue-modal glue-modal--dark QHdDac" role="dialog" aria-modal="true"><img class="JcsBte mZzdH ZOnyjc" src="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_3_Global_distribution_of_x.cpe.max-1400x1400.jpg" alt="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_3_Global_distribution_of_x.cpe.max-1400x1400.jpg" jsname='P3Vluc' jsaction="click:HTIlC" loading="lazy"/><button class="glue-modal__close-btn" tabindex="0" aria-label="Close this modal"></button></section></section></figure><div class="nRhiJb-cHYyed nRhiJb-DbgRPb-R6PoUb-ma6Yeb ZpqjUe"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p>Figure 3: Global distribution of x.cpe</p></div></div></section></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">While the overwhelming majority of the infected devices in the XCPE botnet are Android TV set-top boxes, we observed a number of other infected Android-based IoT devices, including telehealth  devices, karaoke machines, and digital signage devices. </span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Monetization</span><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> </span></h4>
<p><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> loads a variety of malicious modules for monetization. Most often the modules are associated with residential proxy services and bandwidth sharing, but we've also observed ad fraud modules. Given that a single device may be running multiple modules providing the same or similar functionality (e.g., as many as three residential proxy SDKs installed on a single device) it is clear that Videostrong seeks to monetize every infected device to the greatest extent possible. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Services are loaded by </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> via three methods:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Native Daemons</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Native ELF binaries prepackaged with </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, which are unpacked to data directories and spawned as background processes with system/root privileges.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Embedded SDKs / Services</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Java/Kotlin SDKs compiled directly into Nexus and invoked via Android Service lifecycles or IPC broadcast triggers.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Dynamic Droppers &amp; Secondary APK Installers</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Downloader function that pulls remote native ELFs or secondary standalone APKs and invokes system package managers. Nexus receives messages from </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> directing it to download specific modules.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Malware modules loaded via the backdoor include the following: </span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Residential Proxy / Bandwidth Sharing Modules</span></h4>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">LINKFOG</strong></p>
<p><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">’s </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">LinkfogService</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> unpacks a Go binary named </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">lw_android_agent</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">  from </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">assets/</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> to local app storage and supplies it with vendor parameters. This service silently turns infected devices into residential edge computing and PCDN proxy exit nodes. It connects to FRP reverse tunnels, and uploads bandwidth accounting metrics to Linkfog API backends. Linkfog (operated by Beijing Linkfog Technology via 点心云 / Dianxinyun) brands itself as a “Peer-to-Peer Content Delivery Network”.</span></p>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Jaguar</strong></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline"> com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">AdtimeService </code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">extracts </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">libjaguar.so</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> from </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">assets/</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. The file is an unobfuscated 32-bit ELF binary written in C, compiled as a native Android binary. It utilizes JNI (Java Native Interface) to initialize its core routines, dynamically derives a ChaCha20 decryption key via ARM NEON SIMD instructions to decode heavily obfuscated configurations , and establishes an encrypted C2 channel. Upon successful registration, the C2 responds with JSON configuration specifying proxy endpoints. The proxy command loop </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x_ctrl_do_ctrl_msg</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> delegates proxy operations based on incoming JSON variable commands, and is capable of both TCP and UDP relay.</span></p>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">AppSalt / Honeygain</strong></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">AppSalt SDK is the residential proxy SDK owned by Honeygain, which is in turn owned by Oxylabs. </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus.service.CoreService</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> initializes Appsalt SDK. First, the application obtains an Appsalt SDK API key from a native library </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">libnative_keys.so</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> by calling its </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">getAppsaltKey()</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> function. Appsalt SDK is initialized without displaying a consent screen - instead it explicitly calls the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">optIn()</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> method which sets a consent flag without displaying any user interface. During Appsalt SDK startup it checks the application's installation source and stops execution if the application was installed from the official Google Play Store. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Once Appsalt is successfully initialized it establishes a WebSocket connection with the primary command and control domain. Appsalt uses JSON-formatted messages for initial check-in (registration) and heartbeat requests. Appsalt command and control servers use MessagePack formatted messages to issue commands pertaining to proxy operations. Appsalt SDK is configured with an initial set of domains that act as proxy gateways. The list of proxy gateways can be updated by the command and control server. The device establishes UDP / QUIC connections with these gateways.</span></p>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">BWM / IPWeb</strong></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">This residential proxy and bandwidth-sharing node is implemented through the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus.service.BwmService</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> component (sometimes named </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus.service.SN4Service)</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and its associated SDK. Upon initialization, it secures itself within an isolated sandbox environment, captures device telemetry, and registers with a remote server over UDP. From this C2, it receives instructions to initiate a Java NIO TCP multiplexed proxy tunnel. The service functions to route arbitrary traffic through the infected device on behalf of a remote threat actor.</span></p>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">GaGaNode</strong></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">GaGaNode is the first-party SDK owned by residential proxy operator IPCola. It connects over TCP to a primary C2 server and sends heartbeat beacon data over HTTP POST to a separate server. The SDK both contains its own proxy relay functionality, and it initializes additional proxy SDKs from affiliates. Upon device registration to the C2, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.gaganode.sdk.node.endpoint.a</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> receives </span><span style="vertical-align:baseline">incoming packets from relay servers and places them in queue for the client to read and proxy through its exit node socket. The service manages multiple proxy sessions at once (referred to as clients), and each client has its own tcp/udp queue for messages.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">We observe GaGaNode initializing PopaNet/NetNut and EarnFM Fleet residential proxy SDKs.</span><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> GaGaNode/IPCola is thus likely able to further monetize its SDK and offer these affiliates access to the infected exit nodes.</span></p>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">NetNut / Popanet</strong></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Popanet is a residential proxy SDK developed by NetNut, a residential proxy operator. The GaGaNode module initiates the Popanet SDK (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">io.popanet.Popa</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">). This malware is identical to the malware described in our </span><a href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/google-continued-disruption-residential-proxy-networks"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">previous disruption of NetNut</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. All C2 infrastructure used by Popanet samples connected to </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> was taken down as part of the earlier Netnut disruption.</span></p>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Traffmonetizer</strong></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The Traffmonetizer SDK is initialized inside the primary background service </span><span style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus.service.CoreService</span><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. </span><span style="vertical-align:baseline">CoreService</span><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> operation depends on the availability of policy JSON provided by the XCPE backdoor via IPC. Before calling Traffmonetizer SDK initialization methods, the application obtains a Traffmonetizer SDK API key from the native library </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">libnative_keys.so</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> by calling its </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">getTraffmonetizerKey()</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> function. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Traffmonetizer functionality is split between two components where the Android SDK acts as a state machine which handles native library callbacks through asynchronous event dispatchers. </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ServiceBloc</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> monitors connection status updates from the native callbacks and </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ConnectivityBloc</code><strong style="vertical-align:baseline"> </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">monitors Wi-Fi and mobile data network changes. </span></p>
<p style="text-align:justify"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Traffmonetizer native library initialization and configuration is followed by a call to </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">baseClientStart()</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> which establishes a WebSocket connection to a C2 server. Traffmonetizer uses a single WebSocket (TLS) connection to the command and control server to multiplex control messages and tunneled connections (supports both TCP and UDP). Traffmonetizer also supports “Content Delivery” tunneling which allows nodes to tunnel bandwidth-intensive traffic.</span></p>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">EarnFM</strong></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">EarnFM is a residential proxy provider, and Fleet SDK is the provider’s SDK that enrolls devices as exit nodes into the proxy network. In </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, FleetSDK is initialized by </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.gaganode.sdk</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> as mentioned above. FleetSdk then loads native binary </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">libfleetshare.so</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">FleetSDK establishes a persistent WebSocket connection with its primary C2 server for routing arbitrary network traffic. Incoming tasking frames are received over the WebSocket channel and processed in a continuous loop. Messages contain structured JSON commands instructing the daemon to connect to specific remote hosts, with support for both TCP and UDP.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Backdoor Modules</span></h4>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Spirit</strong></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Spirit is a dynamic code loading backdoor. Spirit employs multiple stages. The first stage payload fetches device information (hardware, operating system, and manufacturer/brand information and geolocation), posting it over HTTP to a hardcoded C2 server, and fetches a list of additional modules to download from the server. The second stage communicates with a separate server, and is used for </span><span style="vertical-align:baseline">tasking, config sync, module retrieval, heartbeats, and reporting. </span><span style="vertical-align:baseline">We have observed Spirit loading a significant amount of additional malware monetization modules, including some of the families already directly loaded by </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Moyu</strong></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Moyu is a 3-stage backdoor previously observed as part of the BadBox enterprise. On </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">-backdoored devices, we observed it initialized by </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus.service.DevourService.</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> The first stage is an offline dropper that reconstructs and loads an embedded second stage. Stage two polls an update C2 to download, decrypt, and load the third stage. Stage three implements a backdoor with hidden Double-WebView click/conversion fraud, persistent clipboard hijacking, and secondary dynamically loaded JAR execution and a separate residential proxy trojan.</span></p>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Bird</strong></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Bird is a 2-stage backdoor previously tied to the BadBox enterprise. Stage 1 beacons a device and analysis-environment profile to its C2 server and, when the server approves, downloads, decrypts, and loads the Stage 2 JAR. Stage 2 implements a dynamic code loading backdoor (two plugin loaders plus a C2-driven reflective method invoker), a separate downloader (silent APK installation and Accessibility-driven Google Play installs), and ad abuse (lockscreen ads, overlays, browser launches).</span></p>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Fission</strong></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Fission is a dynamic code loading (DCL) backdoor embedded within standard video applications (e.g., </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.newtv.cboxtv</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">). Upon initialization, the first stage plugin contacts its C2 server to download additional modules. The decrypted second stage payload fetches execution targets and tracking URLs from the C2. The request sends an unencrypted POST request with tracking metrics from the internal </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">DeviceManager.</code></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The payload we observed was configured to commit ad fraud. Rather than rendering visible advertisements in a </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">WebView</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> or UI component, the Stage 2 plugin executes headlessly in the background. The module queries ad server endpoints in the background to retrieve ad placement responses, parses out both primary impression beacons (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">pvm</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) and third-party tracking monitor URLs (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">pvtpm</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">), and immediately fires HTTP GET requests to each tracking endpoint to register a fake ad view without displaying creative content.</span><strong style="vertical-align:baseline"> </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">When executing inside specific smart-TV application stores (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.changhong.appstore</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> or </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.tcl.appmarket2</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">), the plugin initializes and triggers the ad SDK directly to report synthetic ad exposures.</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Ad-Fraud</span></h4>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">HiddenView</strong></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">We observed HiddenView loaded remotely from the attacker’s Github channel as </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.ota.service</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. It is a standalone ad fraud application capable of running in the background using a transparent window overlay, using hidden WebViews to load targeted web pages and simulate user interaction (scrolling, clicking, typing, media playback), playing media unattended, and capturing the user’s screen.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">How the pieces come together</span><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> </span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Configuration messages pushed via MQTT (action </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ext/lib/config</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) demonstrate how </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and various monetization modules function together. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Upon registration, subscribed devices receive a </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ext/lib/config</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> message, which contains configuration parameters for malicious plugins. This includes:</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Limits on system resources:</span></p>
<p> </p>
<pre><code>"constraints": [
    {
      "cpu": 70,
      "ioRead": 5000,
      "ioWrite": 5000,
      "libCode": "linkfog",
      "memory": 50,
      "reportAlarm": true,
      "trigger": 3
    }</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 4: System resource limits configuration payload</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Module name, download location per architecture, hashes, and weight (relative priority for system resources):</span></p>
<p> </p>
<pre><code> "infos": [
    {
      "libChannel": "SZKJ",
      "libCode": "linkfog",
      "libResource": [
        {
          "address": "https://github.com/Aviciiii/ott/releases/download/cpe_ext_lib/lw_android_agent_special_ndk_64",
          "architecture": "arm64-v8a",
          "md5": "abd0b6de6fdf78234f5dab098d5dd229"
        },
        {
          "address": "https://github.com/Aviciiii/ott/releases/download/cpe_ext_lib/lw_android_agent_special_ndk_32_20250717",
          "architecture": "armeabi-v7a",
          "md5": "d8971688d1929b6ed915b80eca7e7e3f"
        }
      ],
      "libWeight": 5
    },</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 5: Module configuration payload</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Devices are assigned to one of six timezone-separated pools on the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ott.szbboys[.]com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> domain:</span></p>
<p> </p>
<pre><code>"region": "Iraq",
  "serverPool": {
    "defaultPool": "Pool5",
    "lastUpdated": "2025-08-06 17:00:00",</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 6: Server Pool configuration payload</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The “</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">policys</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">” section instructs </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> which modules to download and run:</span></p>
<p> </p>
<pre><code> "status": [
    {
      "enable": true,
      "libCode": "linkfog"
    },
    {
      "enable": false,
      "libCode": "bwm"
    },
    {
      "enable": false,
      "libCode": "adtime"
    },
    {
      "enable": false,
      "libCode": "admoyu"
    },
    {
      "enable": false,
      "libCode": "titan"
    },
    {
      "enable": false,
      "libCode": "lb102"
    }
  ],</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 7: Module activation payload</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> passes on these configuration parameters to </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> for the given module on the device. Devices report bandwidth usage and alarms back to </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">serverPool</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> to stay within the configured constraints. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">When </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">com.abc.nexus</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> acknowledges receiving the bundle, </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">x.cpe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> immediately publishes </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ext/lib/config/reply</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> back to the MQTT broker:</span></p>
<p> </p>
<pre><code>"code": 0,
  "msg": "success",
  "data": "received data success and applying",
  "async": true,
  "immediately": false</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 8: x.cpe response payload</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Infrastructure</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">As previously mentioned, devices first connect to various endpoints on </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">*.anyevonline[.]com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> for initial bootstrap, registration, and configuration. Earlier malware versions used various hostnames of </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">{*tms | *ott | *ota}.szbboys[.]com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> for the same purpose. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">While earlier versions of the malware used HTTP for C2, and then a blended C2 approach with both HTTP and MQTT, static analysis of current versions indicate they exclusively send/receive C2 communications via MQTT. A single MQTT broker orchestrates all MQTT C2 communications for samples we’ve identified. Hostname </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">mqtt.szbboys[.]com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> is resolved by hosts</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline"> 219.134.163[.]210</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">219.134.163[.]211. </code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">It listens on non-standard MQTT ports 3885-3887. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The malware constructs OkHttp requests for file downloads after tasking via the MQTT broker. Files are hosted on several services:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Several Github release pages</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">filemail[.]com</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> file sharing service</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Two operator-controlled servers hosted on IPs</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline"> 38.145.197[.]135 </code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">and </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">23.94.49[.]57</code></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Netflow telemetry on the broker nodes (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">219.134.163[.]210/.211</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) indicates that while infected devices communicate over ports 3885–3887, operators likely manage the broker and dispatch tasking via interactive HTTP REST API (port 8082), WebSocket (port 8083), and EMQX web administration console (port 18083) sessions originating from non-bot IP space. </span></p>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Call to Action</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">While we believe our actions have seriously impacted over a dozen malicious actors, the underlying illicit market is resilient. Backdoor operators, ad-fraud rings, and residential proxy providers operate as an interconnected supply chain, and when one component is disrupted, operators quickly pivot to new loaders or rival monetization SDKs. Creating lasting change requires accountability across the hardware supply chain, the residential proxy industry, and the broader technology ecosystem.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Accountability and Reform</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">A device manufacturer intentionally infecting their users with malware is an unacceptable breach of trust. By turning everyday hardware into persistent beachheads for criminal affiliates, Videostrong prioritized illicit post-sale recurring revenue over the safety and privacy of its users. Downstream hardware brands, white-label resellers, telecommunications providers, and online e-commerce marketplaces also share responsibility for closing this vector. Retailers and distributors should require independent security audits from Original Design Manufacturers (ODMs) and swiftly delist manufacturers implicated in supply-chain abuse.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Residential proxy providers have been able to flourish under the guise of legitimate businesses. While some providers may claim to behave ethically and only enroll devices with the clear consent of consumers, any claims of "ethical sourcing" must be backed by transparent, auditable proof of user consent. In our analysis, these proxy operators only seek consent when they are forced due to platform policy and enforcement. Silent opt-in without user consent remains the most prevalent means by which devices are turned into exit nodes.  </span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Empowering and Protecting the Consumer</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Residential proxies are an understudied area of risk for consumers, and more can be done to raise awareness. Consumers should be extremely wary of applications that offer payment in exchange for "unused bandwidth" or "sharing your internet." These applications are primary ways for illicit proxy networks to grow, and could open security vulnerabilities on the device’s home network. We urge users to stick to app stores you trust, review permissions for third-party VPNs and proxies, and ensure built-in security protections like Google Play Protect are active.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Consumers should only purchase connected devices, such as set-top boxes, from reputable manufacturers. Our </span><a href="https://www.android.com/certified/partners/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">Android TV website</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> provides the most up-to-date list of partners.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Industry Collaboration</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">We encourage mobile platforms, ISPs, online marketplaces and other tech platforms to continue sharing intelligence and implementing best practices to identify malicious backdoors, illicit proxy networks, and other harmful software and limit their harms.</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Indicators of Compromise</span></h3>

Intelligence\"],\"timeRequired\":\"PT76M\"}\u003c/script\u003e"],["Shutting the Door on the XCPE Botnet"],null,null,[[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003ch3\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eIntroduction\u003c/span\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e \u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eLast week, Google and industry partners dismantled the XCPE backdoor which enrolled more than 1.3 million set-top boxes into a botnet. We assess with high confidence that the backdoor and botnet were intentionally developed, deployed, and operated for financial gain by Videostrong Technology Co., an Android Original Equipment Manufacturer (OEM) and Original Design Manufacturer (ODM).\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eVideostrong monetized the backdoor by selling access to criminal affiliates and loading those affiliates’ malware modules onto infected devices. These secondary modules were conducting ad-fraud; silently enrolling infected devices into residential proxy networks; and loading additional malware onto XCPE-compromised devices. We also took action to disrupt the command and control (C2) domains that operate these affiliate modules. Because these affiliates each have diversified distribution channels for their modules beyond XCPE, we assess that taking down their C2 domains will sever access to at least 10 million additional compromised devices. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eWe found this malicious software built directly into many different device models and brands over multiple years, supported by dedicated infrastructure and constantly evolving configurations to evade detection and manage their affiliates. We believe this campaign wasn’t an isolated rogue insider or a development oversight. As a result, Google has cut ties with Videostrong and banned them from making devices with preloaded Google apps. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eGoogle requires OEMs go through a comprehensive suite of compatibility and security tests to ensure a consistent and safe user experience across the Android ecosystem. However, Videostrong employed obfuscation techniques to exploit a gap in our tests and load malicious code after the device's first boot. As a result, Google has implemented changes to its existing review process to better safeguard against similar activity in the future. A device manufacturer intentionally infecting their users with malware is an unacceptable breach of trust, and represents a serious risk to those users. Without users’ knowledge or consent, infected devices were offered as a distribution mechanism for a wide array of criminal actors. This led to a variety of malicious activities being conducted on and through the devices, including ad-fraud, data exfiltration, and \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/google-continued-disruption-residential-proxy-networks?e\u003d48754805\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003erouting malicious network traffic\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e through the devices.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eWe worked closely with Japan’s National Police Agency, Lumen, HUMAN Security, Plume, Synthient, and TrendAI to analyze this malware ecosystem and disrupt its operations. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eWhile we expect this disruption to have a significant effect on these criminal operations, this is not the end of our work combatting malicious activity. Backdoor operators, ad-fraud rings, and residential proxy providers operate as an interconnected supply chain. Point-in-time disruptions are a critical tool to protect our users, but  lasting disruption in this fluid ecosystem will require continued targeting of this infrastructure. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eGoogle undertook the following actions to support disruption of the XCPE botnet: \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eLegal action to take down the domains and servers Videostrong used to control the botnet and install malware modules\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eLegal action to take down C2 domains used by over a dozen malware families loaded as modules via this backdoor\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eBanned Videostrong and any future subsidiaries or affiliates from building and selling certified Android devices in the future, or supplying software to other certified vendors\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eImplementing improvements to how we ensure the integrity of firmware on certified Android TV devices \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eOverview\u003c/span\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThe x.cpe network was built for ongoing profit. By giving malware affiliates backdoor access, the operators conspired to monetize these devices long after purchase. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"]]],[null,null,null,null,null,null,null,null,[[[null,"\u003cp\u003eFigure 1: XCPE malware overview\u003c/p\u003e"],["https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_1_XCPE_malware_overview.max-1600x1600.png","https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/Figure_1_XCPE_malware_overview.max-1100x1100.png
